<section class="view" id="v-arsenal" data-view="arsenal">
  <header class="hero">
    <p class="eyebrow">Part 3 · after <a href="/blog/explainers/the-cell-that-forgot-to-stop/">The Cell That Forgot to Stop</a> and <a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/">The Cancer Atlas</a></p>
    <h1>The Cancer <span>Arsenal</span></h1>
    <p class="dek">Every treatment, and the molecules behind them: what each one hits, why it works, why it hurts, and why cancer sometimes escapes it.</p>
  </header>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">The big picture</span><h3 class="sec">Where each weapon strikes</h3></div>

    <figcaption>Part 1 described a cancer cell as a car with a stuck accelerator, cut brakes and no mechanics. Every treatment either removes the car, smashes its engine (DNA), jams the accelerator, exploits the missing mechanics, or calls the police (the immune system). Tap a name to open that chapter.</figcaption>

    <figcaption>A tumor board of surgeons, medical and radiation oncologists, radiologists and pathologists agrees the plan. Drugs given before surgery (neoadjuvant) shrink the tumor and show whether it responds; drugs given after (adjuvant) kill stray cells that scans cannot see.</figcaption></figure>
  <div class="outc">
    <div style="border-top-color:var(--teal)"><b>Cure</b><span>The goal when cancer is local, and for many blood cancers. Surgery, radiation and chemo do most of the curing.</span></div>
    <div style="border-top-color:var(--hema)"><b>Control</b><span>When cancer has spread, many can be held in check for years, like a chronic disease. Hormone, targeted and immunotherapy shine here.</span></div>
    <div style="border-top-color:var(--eosin)"><b>Comfort</b><span>Relieving pain and symptoms. Useful at every stage, not only at the end.</span></div>
  </div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Which tool for which cancer</span><h3 class="sec">The treatment map</h3></div>
  <figure class="plate">

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Reading the label</span><h3 class="sec">Decode a drug name</h3></div>
  <div class="pair">
    <div class="decoder">
      <p style="font-size:17px">Drug names are not random. The ending tells you what kind of molecule it is and how it works. Type any cancer drug name:</p>
      <label class="mono-l" for="dec-in">Drug name</label>
      <input id="dec-in" type="text" placeholder="e.g. osimertinib" autocomplete="off" spellcheck="false" value="pembrolizumab">
      <p class="dec-out" id="dec-out" aria-live="polite"></p>
    </div>
    <aside class="note"><b>Two clues in one name</b><p>In pembroli·zu·mab, "mab" says monoclonal antibody and "zu" says it was humanised: built from a mouse antibody with most parts swapped for human ones, so the body does not reject it.</p></aside>
  </div>
  <div class="sfx"><div><code>-mab</code> monoclonal antibody<br><i>pembrolizumab, trastuzumab</i></div><div><code>-ximab / -zumab / -umab</code> mouse-human / humanised / fully human antibody (older naming)<br><i>rituximab, bevacizumab, daratumumab</i></div><div><code>-nib</code> small-molecule kinase inhibitor (-tinib: tyrosine kinase)<br><i>imatinib, osimertinib</i></div><div><code>-ciclib</code> CDK inhibitor<br><i>palbociclib, ribociclib</i></div><div><code>-parib</code> PARP inhibitor<br><i>olaparib, niraparib</i></div><div><code>-rasib</code> RAS inhibitor<br><i>sotorasib, daraxonrasib</i></div><div><code>-lisib</code> PI3K inhibitor<br><i>alpelisib, gedatolisib</i></div><div><code>-toclax</code> BCL-2 inhibitor<br><i>venetoclax</i></div><div><code>-zomib</code> proteasome inhibitor<br><i>bortezomib, carfilzomib</i></div><div><code>-platin</code> platinum chemotherapy<br><i>cisplatin, oxaliplatin</i></div><div><code>-taxel</code> taxane spindle poison<br><i>paclitaxel, docetaxel</i></div><div><code>-rubicin</code> anthracycline<br><i>doxorubicin, epirubicin</i></div><div><code>-tecan</code> topoisomerase I poison (also ADC payloads)<br><i>irinotecan, deruxtecan</i></div><div><code>-vedotin / -deruxtecan / -govitecan</code> the payload of an antibody-drug conjugate<br><i>enfortumab vedotin</i></div><div><code>-rozole</code> aromatase inhibitor<br><i>letrozole, anastrozole</i></div><div><code>-lutamide</code> androgen receptor blocker<br><i>enzalutamide, apalutamide</i></div><div><code>-relin / -relix</code> GnRH agonist / antagonist<br><i>goserelin, degarelix</i></div><div><code>-cel</code> cell therapy<br><i>tisagenlecleucel</i></div><div><code>-vec / -repvec</code> virus-based gene therapy<br><i>talimogene laherparepvec, vusolimogene oderparepvec</i></div><div><code>-gestrant / -estrant</code> oestrogen receptor degrader<br><i>fulvestrant, vepdegestrant</i></div></div>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">130 years in 15 steps</span><h3 class="sec">How the arsenal was built</h3></div>
  <ol class="tl"><li><span class="yr">1894</span><b>Radical mastectomy</b><span>Halsted's operation sets the template for cancer surgery</span></li><li><span class="yr">1896</span><b>First radiation treatment</b><span>a year after Röntgen discovers X-rays</span></li><li><span class="yr">1948</span><b>First chemotherapy remissions</b><span>Farber, childhood leukaemia</span></li><li><span class="yr">1977</span><b>Tamoxifen approved</b><span>the first targeted hormone drug for breast cancer</span></li><li><span class="yr">1981</span><b>Hepatitis B vaccine</b><span>the first vaccine that prevents a cancer</span></li><li><span class="yr">1997</span><b>Rituximab</b><span>the first antibody drug for cancer</span></li><li><span class="yr">2001</span><b>Imatinib</b><span>a pill turns CML into a chronic condition</span></li><li><span class="yr">2006</span><b>HPV vaccine</b><span>cervical cancer becomes preventable</span></li><li><span class="yr">2011</span><b>Ipilimumab</b><span>first checkpoint inhibitor</span></li><li><span class="yr">2014</span><b>Pembrolizumab</b><span>PD-1 blockade; later the world's best-selling drug</span></li><li><span class="yr">2017</span><b>First CAR-T</b><span>a living drug for leukaemia</span></li><li><span class="yr">2019</span><b>Trastuzumab deruxtecan</b><span>the ADC era takes off</span></li><li><span class="yr">2022</span><b>Pluvicto</b><span>guided radiation for prostate cancer</span></li><li><span class="yr">2023</span><b>NexCAR19</b><span>India's own CAR-T</span></li><li><span class="yr">2026</span><b>First PROTAC and pan-RAS drug</b><span>vepdegestrant; daraxonrasib for pancreatic cancer</span></li></ol>

  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Money and access</span><h3 class="sec">Who pays, and how India changes the price</h3></div>
  <div class="money">
    <div><b>$31.7 billion</b><span>Keytruda (pembrolizumab) sales in 2025, the world's best-selling medicine. Its key patents expire in 2028, opening the door to cheaper biosimilars.</span></div>
    <div><b>~$50,000</b><span>Expected cost of India's NexCAR19 CAR-T, against about $400,000 for CAR-T in the US.</span></div>
    <div><b>2013</b><span>India's Supreme Court refused Novartis a patent on a new form of imatinib, keeping generic versions cheap for CML patients.</span></div>
    <div><b>Biosimilars</b><span>Indian companies make copies of antibody drugs such as trastuzumab, rituximab and bevacizumab once patents expire, often at a fraction of the original price.</span></div>
  </div>
  <p class="prose" style="font-size:15px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Numbers from Merck's reported sales (via BioSpace) and the US National Cancer Institute. This is general information, not financial advice.</p>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-surgery" data-view="surgery" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 1 of 10 · Cut it out · since 1880s</p>
    <h2>Surgery</h2>
    <p class="thesis">The oldest cancer treatment, and still the one most likely to cure a solid tumor that has not spread.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>an operation, open or keyhole/robotic</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>solid tumors caught before they spread</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Can't do</b><span>reach cancer that is already in the blood</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>The goal is to remove the whole tumor with a <strong>rim of healthy tissue</strong> around it, called the margin, so no cancer cells are left at the cut edge. After the operation a pathologist paints the outside of the specimen with ink and looks at it under the microscope. If cancer touches the ink, the margin is positive and more treatment is needed.</p><p>Surgeons also check the lymph nodes, the first stop for spreading cells. Instead of removing all of them, they now often inject a dye or tracer near the tumor and follow it to the first node it reaches, the <em class="term">sentinel node</em>. If that node is clean, the others usually are too, which spares patients painful arm or leg swelling.</p><p>Surgery has become much gentler. Keyhole (laparoscopic) and robotic operations use small cuts. Breast-conserving surgery plus radiation now cures as well as removing the whole breast for most early cancers.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Surgery</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 270" role="img" aria-label="Left: a tumor removed with a rim of healthy tissue, and dye flowing through a lymph channel to the first lymph node, the sentinel node. the sentinel node. Right: what the pathologist checks.</figcaption></figure>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Pain, infection and bleeding, like any operation</li><li>Loss of function of what was removed (part of the bowel, a voice box, a breast)</li><li>Lymphoedema: swelling of an arm or leg if many nodes are removed</li><li>Fatigue for weeks as the body heals</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Cannot remove cells that have already spread through the blood, which is why surgery is often combined with drugs before or after</li><li>Some tumors sit too close to vital structures to remove safely</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#oral">Mouth</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#cervical">Cervix</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#lung">Lung</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#colorectal">Colorectal</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#liver">Liver</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#skin">Skin</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>
  
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Surgery or a biopsy makes cancer spread.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>There is no good evidence of this. Biopsies are planned to avoid spillage, and delaying diagnosis is far more dangerous.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#arsenal">← The arsenal</a><a href="#radiation">Radiation therapy →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-radiation" data-view="radiation" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 2 of 10 · Break the DNA from outside · since 1896</p>
    <h2>Radiation therapy</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Precisely aimed high-energy rays that shatter cancer DNA. About half of all people with cancer receive it at some point.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>daily sessions of a few minutes, usually over 1 to 7 weeks</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>local tumors, and shrinking painful spread</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Can't do</b><span>treat the whole body at once</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Radiation (usually X-ray photons from a machine called a linear accelerator) snaps both strands of DNA. A cell with too many breaks dies when it next tries to divide.</p><p>Two tricks protect healthy tissue. First, <strong>geometry</strong>: many beams from different angles cross at the tumor, so only the crossing point gets a high dose, and computers shape each beam to the tumor's outline (IMRT). Second, <strong>time</strong>: the dose is split into small daily fractions. Healthy cells repair much of the damage overnight; many cancer cells have broken repair genes (Part 1) and fall behind with each dose.</p><p>Radiation can also go <em>inside</em> the body. In <strong>brachytherapy</strong> a radioactive source is placed in or next to the tumor for minutes, the standard for cervical cancer. Proton beams stop at a set depth, useful near the brain or in children.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Radiation therapy</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 312" role="img" aria-label="Left: a body cross-section with six radiation beams crossing at the tumor, so the dose is high only where they overlap; the spinal cord is spared. Right: an illustrative chart over six daily doses: healthy tissue dips and recovers between doses, while the tumor steps down."><defs><marker id="ard" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" dose</text><line class="line soft" x1="80" y1="120" x2="30" y2="24"/><text class="s" x="14" y="18">each beam alone: low dose</text><line class="axis" x1="430" y1="240" x2="750" y2="240"/><line text-anchor="middle">treatment days (one small dose a day)</text><text class="m" x="430" y="38">cells still alive (illustrative)</text><polyline class="ln teal" points="430.0,60.0 432.0,110.4 483.3,70.8 485.3,118.2 536.7,78.6 538.7,123.8 590.0,84.2 592.0,127.8 643.3,88.2 645.3,130.7 696.7,91.1 698.7,132.8 750.0,93.2"/><polyline class="ln red" points="430.0,60.0 432.0,128.4 483.3,124.8 485.3,168.6 536.7,165.0 538.7,193.5 590.0,189.9 592.0,208.9 643.3,205.3 645.3,218.5 696.7,214.9 698.7,224.4 750.0,220.8"/><text class="s teal" x="754" y="87.19788589056003" text-anchor="end">healthy</text><text class="s red" x="754" y="236.84038087936" text-anchor="end">tumor</text><text class="s" x="560" y="300" text-anchor="middle">healthy cells repair overnight; many tumor cells cannot</text></svg></div><figcaption>Geometry and time: beams cross only at the tumor, and daily small doses let healthy tissue recover while the tumor falls behind.</figcaption></figure>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Effects are mostly local to the area treated</li><li>Skin redness like sunburn; tiredness</li><li>Head and neck: dry mouth, sore throat, taste loss</li><li>Pelvis: diarrhoea, bladder irritation</li><li>Late effects months to years later: scarring, and a small risk of a new cancer</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Each organ has a lifetime dose limit, so the same area can rarely be fully treated twice</li><li>Tumors with low oxygen resist it</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#oral">Mouth</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#cervical">Cervix</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#lung">Lung</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#colorectal">Colorectal</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#liver">Liver</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#skin">Skin</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>
  
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Radiation therapy makes you radioactive.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>External beam radiation does not. You can hug your family right after. Only some implanted sources and radioactive drugs need brief precautions.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#surgery">← Surgery</a><a href="#chemotherapy">Chemotherapy →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-chemotherapy" data-view="chemotherapy" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 3 of 10 · Poison dividing cells · since 1940s</p>
    <h2>Chemotherapy</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Drugs that kill cells in the act of dividing. Crude but powerful, and still the backbone of curing leukaemia, lymphoma and testicular cancer.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>IV drips or pills, in cycles every 1 to 4 weeks</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>fast-growing cancers; mopping up cells after surgery</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Main downside</b><span>it also hits healthy dividing cells</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Chemotherapy began with a tragedy: sailors exposed to mustard gas in 1943 had their white blood cells wiped out, which suggested a drug against lymphoma. In 1948 Sidney Farber produced the first remissions in childhood leukaemia with aminopterin.</p><p>Every chemo drug attacks some part of cell division, so each class strikes at a different point of the cell cycle from Part 1. Doctors combine drugs from different classes so that a cell resistant to one is still killed by another. That combination idea is what made childhood ALL about 90% curable.</p><p>Chemo is given in <strong>cycles</strong> because it also hits healthy fast-dividing cells, especially the bone marrow. Counts fall after each dose and must recover before the next.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Chemotherapy</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 330" role="img" aria-label="The cell cycle ring with four chemotherapy classes pinned where they strike: antimetabolites during S phase when DNA is copied, topoisomerase blockers around S and G2, spindle poisons during M phase, and alkylators and platinums that cross-link DNA in any phase."><defs><marker id="acc" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bcc" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><g transform="rotate(-90 210 170)"><circle class="seg g1" cx="210" cy="170" r="120" stroke-dasharray="297.6 456.4" stroke-dashoffset="0"/><circle class="seg sp" cx="210" cy="170" r="120" dose.</figcaption></figure>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Hair loss: hair follicles divide fast</li><li>Mouth sores, diarrhoea: the gut lining renews every few days</li><li>Infections, anaemia, bruising: the marrow is hit</li><li>Nausea and vomiting (now well controlled)</li><li>Tingling or numbness in hands and feet (taxanes, platinums)</li><li>Drug-specific: heart (doxorubicin), kidneys and hearing (cisplatin), fertility</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Cancer cells can pump the drug out or repair the damage</li><li>Slow-growing cancers barely respond</li><li>Toxicity caps the dose</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#oral">Mouth</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#cervical">Cervix</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#lung">Lung</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#colorectal">Colorectal</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#liver">Liver</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#skin">Skin</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>

dna (cross-links) generic breast blood"><h4>Cyclophosphamide</h4><span class="br">Endoxan</span><div class="row"><span class="cl">Chemotherapy</span><span class="rt">IV / pill</span></div><p class="tg"><b>Target:</b> DNA (cross-links)</p><p class="nt">Descended from mustard gas research in World War II.</p><div class="row cx"><a topoisomerase i pfizer · generic colorectal stomach"><h4>Irinotecan</h4><span class="br">Camptosar</span><div class="row"><span class="cl">Chemotherapy</span><span class="rt">IV</span></div><p class="tg"><b>Target:</b> Topoisomerase I</p><p class="nt">Derived from a Chinese 'happy tree', Camptotheca.</p><div class="row cx"><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#colorectal">Colorectal</a><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a></div><p class="meta">first approved 1996 · Pfizer · generic</p></article></div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Chemo is so toxic it does more harm than good.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>For many cancers it is the difference between cure and death. Side effects are mostly temporary and far better managed than 20 years ago.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#radiation">← Radiation therapy</a><a href="#hormone">Hormone therapy →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-hormone" data-view="hormone" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 4 of 10 · Cut off the fuel · since 1890s</p>
    <h2>Hormone therapy</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Some cancers still run on the body's hormones. Starve them of oestrogen or testosterone and they shrink, sometimes for years.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>daily pills or monthly injections, often for 5 to 10 years</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>hormone-receptor-positive breast cancer, prostate cancer</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Main downside</b><span>menopause-like or low-testosterone symptoms</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>In 1896 a Scottish surgeon removed the ovaries of women with advanced breast cancer and saw tumors shrink; in 1941 Charles Huggins did the same for prostate cancer by lowering testosterone, and later won a Nobel Prize.</p><p>Today's drugs block the hormone pipeline at different points: stop the brain's signal, stop production, block the receptor, or destroy the receptor. In 2026 the first <strong>PROTAC</strong>, vepdegestrant, was approved: it tags the oestrogen receptor for the cell's own shredder.</p><p>Taking tamoxifen or an aromatase inhibitor for 5 to 10 years after surgery cuts the risk of breast cancer coming back by roughly a third to a half.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Hormone therapy</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 340" role="img" aria-label="Two hormone pipelines with block points. Breast: ovaries and fat make oestrogen, which reaches the ER receptor and switches on growth genes; letrozole stops production in fat, tamoxifen blocks the receptor, fulvestrant and vepdegestrant destroy it. Prostate: a brain signal orders testosterone production, which reaches the androgen receptor; leuprolide silences the signal, abiraterone stops production, enzalutamide blocks the receptor."><defs><marker id="ahm" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bhm" them.</figcaption></figure>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Breast: hot flushes, joint pain, bone thinning (aromatase inhibitors); small risk of clots (tamoxifen)</li><li>Prostate: hot flushes, loss of sex drive, muscle and bone loss, weight gain</li><li>Mood changes and tiredness</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Tumors evolve around the block: the receptor mutates (ESR1) or the cell makes more of it</li><li>Useless for receptor-negative cancers, such as triple-negative breast cancer</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">The molecules</span><h3 class="sec">Key drugs in this class</h3></div><div class="mol"><article class="mc" data-cls="hormone" data-q="tamoxifen nolvadex class="tg"><b>Target:</b> Oestrogen receptor (blocks)</p><p class="nt">On the WHO essential list; costs a few rupees a day.</p><div class="row cx"><a class="nt">Blocks testosterone made by the adrenals and the tumor itself.</p><div class="row cx"><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a></div><p class="meta">first approved   <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Hormone therapy means hormone pills like the contraceptive.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>It does the opposite: it removes or blocks hormones rather than adding them.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#chemotherapy">← Chemotherapy</a><a href="#targeted">Targeted therapy →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-targeted" data-view="targeted" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 5 of 10 · Hit the broken part · since 1998</p>
    <h2>Targeted therapy</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Drugs designed against the exact accelerator a tumor depends on. When the target is right, the effect can be dramatic; the catch is resistance.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>daily pills (-nib, -rasib) or antibody drips (-mab)</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>tumors with a known driver, found by biomarker tests</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Main downside</b><span>tumors evolve resistance</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Targeted therapy only works if the tumor has the target, so it starts with a <strong>biomarker test</strong> on the biopsy: is there an EGFR mutation, an ALK fusion, extra HER2? This is why modern pathology reports read like gene lists.</p><p>There are two shapes of drug. <strong>Small molecules</strong> (names ending -nib, -ciclib, -parib, -rasib) are pills small enough to enter the cell and jam an enzyme from inside. <strong>Monoclonal antibodies</strong> (ending -mab) are large Y-shaped proteins given by drip or injection; they grab a target on the outside of the cell.</p><p>A clever twist is <strong>synthetic lethality</strong>. A cell has two main roads to repair DNA breaks. BRCA-mutant cancers have lost one; PARP inhibitors such as olaparib close the other. Normal cells survive, BRCA cancer cells die.</p><p>In 2021 sotorasib became the first drug to hit KRAS, the accelerator from Part 1 that was called "undruggable" for 40 years. In 2026 daraxonrasib, which grabs the active form of many RAS variants, became the first RAS drug for pancreatic cancer.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Targeted therapy</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 290" role="img" aria-label="Left: a growth receptor crossing the cell membrane. An antibody blocks it from outside; a kinase-inhibitor pill jams its kinase inside, so the grow signal stops. Right: synthetic lethality grid. A normal cell survives a PARP inhibitor because BRCA repair still works; a BRCA-broken cancer cell survives without the drug using PARP repair, but dies when the PARP inhibitor closes its last repair road."><defs><marker id="att" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="btt" and why doctors re-biopsy and switch drugs.</figcaption></figure>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Depend on where else the target lives in the body</li><li>EGFR blockers: acne-like rash, diarrhoea (skin and gut use EGFR)</li><li>HER2 blockers: weaker heart pumping (heart muscle uses HER2)</li><li>VEGF blockers: high blood pressure, slow wound healing</li><li>CDK4/6 and PARP inhibitors: low blood counts</li><li>BTK inhibitors: bruising, irregular heartbeat</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Resistance: a new mutation in the target (EGFR T790M) or a bypass road</li><li>Only helps patients whose tumor has the target</li><li>Many tumors have no druggable driver yet</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#lung">Lung</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#colorectal">Colorectal</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#liver">Liver</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#skin">Skin</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#oral">Mouth</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#cervical">Cervix</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>

  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Targeted therapy has no side effects.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Side effects are different, not absent. They are usually milder than chemo, but taken daily for years they add up.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#hormone">← Hormone therapy</a><a href="#immunotherapy">Immunotherapy →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-immunotherapy" data-view="immunotherapy" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 6 of 10 · Release the immune system · since 2011</p>
    <h2>Immunotherapy</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Instead of attacking the cancer directly, these treatments free or arm your own T cells to do it. A minority of patients with advanced cancer now live for years, some apparently cured.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>drips every 3 to 6 weeks; CAR-T is a single infusion</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>melanoma, lung, kidney, bladder, some blood cancers</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Main downside</b><span>immune attack on healthy organs</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Part 1 showed T cells hunting abnormal cells. Cancers survive partly by pulling the T cell's own safety brakes, the <strong>checkpoints</strong>. The main one is PD-1: when a tumor's PD-L1 binds it, the T cell stands down. Checkpoint inhibitors such as pembrolizumab and nivolumab block that handshake. James Allison and Tasuku Honjo won the 2018 Nobel Prize for this idea.</p><p>They work best in tumors with many mutations (melanoma, smokers' lung cancer, MSI-high bowel cancer), because more mutations mean more strange proteins for T cells to recognise.</p><p><strong>CAR-T therapy</strong> goes further: a patient's T cells are taken out, given an engineered receptor (the CAR) that locks onto a marker like CD19 on leukaemia and lymphoma cells, multiplied, and infused back. One infusion can clear cancers that resisted everything else. India built its own: NexCAR19 (2023) costs a fraction of US prices, and a second, Qartemi, followed in 2025.</p><p><strong>Bispecific antibodies</strong> are off-the-shelf cousins: one arm grabs a T cell, the other a cancer cell, and they are pulled together.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Immunotherapy</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 236" role="img" aria-label="Two panels. Left: a T cell recognises a cancer cell, but the cancer's PD-L1 binds the T cell's PD-1 and says stand down. Right: an antibody blocks PD-1, the brake is released and the T cell releases killing granules into the cancer cell."><defs><marker id="ack" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bck" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><circle class="tcell" cx="70" cy="120" r="56"/><circle class="tnuc" cx="62" cy="120" r="30"/><circle class="cellbody" cx="270" cy="120" r="66"/><circle class="nucleus" cx="284" cy="120" r="30"/><rect class="tcr" x="126" y="84" width="40" height="10" rx="4"/><rect class="mhc" x="166" y="84" width="40" height="10" rx="4"/><text class="s" x="166" y="76" text-anchor="middle">recognises the cancer</text><rect class="pd1b" x="126" y="146" width="40" height="10" rx="4"/><rect class="pdl1b" x="166" y="146" width="40" height="10" rx="4"/><text class="t b red" x="166" y="176" text-anchor="middle">PD-1 meets PD-L1:</text><text class="s" x="166" y="191" text-anchor="middle">"stand down"</text><text class="t b" x="70" y="24" text-anchor="middle">T cell</text><text class="t b" x="270" y="24" text-anchor="middle">cancer cell</text><circle class="tcell" cx="470" cy="120" r="56"/><circle class="tnuc"   <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Checkpoint inhibitors: the immune system can attack any organ, causing colitis, lung inflammation, hepatitis, skin rash, or an under- or over-active thyroid</li><li>Most are treated with steroids; a few (thyroid, type 1 diabetes) are permanent</li><li>CAR-T: cytokine release syndrome (high fever, low blood pressure) and confusion within days</li><li>CAR-T and bispecifics: low antibody levels and infections</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Only about 20 to 40% of patients respond to checkpoint inhibitors in most cancers</li><li>'Cold' tumors with few T cells inside barely respond</li><li>CAR-T is complex and expensive, and the cancer can lose its CD19 marker</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#lung">Lung</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#liver">Liver</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#skin">Skin</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#oral">Mouth</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#cervical">Cervix</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#colorectal">Colorectal</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>

  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Immunotherapy is natural, so it is gentle.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>It can cause serious inflammation of the bowel, lungs, liver or heart. Report any new symptom quickly, even months later.</p></div>

<section class="view" id="v-smart" data-view="smart" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 7 of 10 · Deliver poison or radiation to the door · since 2000</p>
    <h2>Smart bombs</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Antibody-drug conjugates and radioligands carry a payload far too toxic to give on its own, straight to cells that wear a particular marker.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>IV drips every 3 to 6 weeks</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>breast, bladder and prostate cancer, growing fast</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Main downside</b><span>the payload can still leak to healthy tissue</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>An <strong>antibody-drug conjugate (ADC)</strong> is three parts: an antibody that finds a marker on the cancer cell, a chemical linker, and a payload of chemotherapy hundreds of times more potent than standard drugs. The cell swallows the antibody, the linker is cut, and the poison is released inside. Newer payloads also leak into neighbouring cells, the <strong>bystander effect</strong>, which lets trastuzumab deruxtecan work even on tumors with only a little HER2.</p><p>A <strong>radioligand</strong> swaps the poison for a radioactive atom. Pluvicto pairs a small molecule that fits PSMA, a marker on prostate cancer cells, with lutetium-177. It circulates through the body and lodges in tumors wherever they are, delivering radiation that reaches only a millimetre or two.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Smart bombs</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 216" role="img" aria-label="Left: an antibody-drug conjugate, an antibody joined by a linker to a toxic payload. Right: four steps: it binds its target on a cancer cell, the cell swallows it, the linker is cut and the payload freed, and the cell dies along with some neighbours."><defs><marker id="aad" viewBox="0 d="M14,5 l3,-7 l3,7 l-7,-4 h8 z"/></g><text class="t b" x="450" y="190" text-anchor="middle">2 · Swallowed</text><text class="s" x="450" y="206" text-anchor="middle">pulled inside</text><circle class="cellbody" cx="560" cy="120" r="44"/><path class="payload" d="M546,112 l3,-7 l3,7 l-7,-4 h8 z"/><path class="payload" d="M558,122 l3,-7 l3,7 l-7,-4 h8 z"/><path class="payload" d="M570,112 l3,-7 l3,7 l-7,-4 h8 z"/><text class="t b" x="560" y="190" text-anchor="middle">3 · Released</text><text class="s" x="560" y="206" text-anchor="middle">poison freed</text><circle class="cellbody" cx="670" cy="120" r="44"/><path class="crack" d="M660,80 l8,16 l-8,10 l12,18 l-6,16"/><circle class="sencell" cx="714" cy="160" r="12"/><path class="crack" d="M710,152 l6,8 l-4,8"/><text class="t b" x="670"   <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>ADCs: low blood counts, nausea, hair loss, and payload-specific effects such as lung inflammation (deruxtecan) or nerve damage (vedotin)</li><li>Radioligands: dry mouth (salivary glands also carry PSMA), tiredness, low blood counts</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Only works if enough cells carry the marker</li><li>Resistance to the payload can still develop</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#cervical">Cervix</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#lung">Lung</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a><a class="u1" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>

  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Targeted means it only touches cancer cells.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Markers are also on some healthy cells, and some payload escapes. These drugs are kinder than chemo but not side-effect free.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#immunotherapy">← Immunotherapy</a><a href="#transplant">Stem cell transplant →</a></div>

<section class="view" id="v-transplant" data-view="transplant" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 8 of 10 · Rebuild the marrow · since 1968</p>
    <h2>Stem cell transplant</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Wipe out the bone marrow with very high doses of treatment, then rescue the patient with new blood-forming stem cells.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>a hospital stay of several weeks</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>leukaemia, lymphoma, myeloma</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Main downside</b><span>weeks without immunity; graft-versus-host disease</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Some blood cancers need doses of chemotherapy so high they would destroy the marrow for good. A transplant makes that possible: the marrow is wiped out, then stem cells are infused into a vein and find their own way back into the bones.</p><p>In an <strong>autologous</strong> transplant the stem cells are the patient's own, collected before treatment, usually for myeloma and lymphoma. In an <strong>allogeneic</strong> transplant they come from a matched donor, usually a sibling or a registry volunteer. The donor's immune cells also attack leftover leukaemia, the <strong>graft-versus-leukaemia</strong> effect, which is often what really cures it.</p><p>E. Donnall Thomas, who pioneered the method, won the 1990 Nobel Prize.</p></div>

working.</figcaption></figure>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Severe infections while counts are near zero</li><li>Mouth and gut sores from the high-dose chemo</li><li>Graft-versus-host disease (allogeneic): donor cells attack the skin, gut or liver</li><li>Infertility; long recovery of strength</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Needs a matched donor for allogeneic transplants (registries help; half-matched family donors now work too)</li><li>Risky for older or frail patients</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>
  
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Bone marrow donation means painful surgery on the spine.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Most donations today are like a long blood donation: stem cells are coaxed into the blood with injections and filtered out.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#smart">← Smart bombs</a><a href="#supportive">Supportive &amp; palliative care →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-supportive" data-view="supportive" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 9 of 10 · Treat the person · since always</p>
    <h2>Supportive &amp; palliative care</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Medicines and care that control symptoms and side effects. Not giving up: started early, it improves quality of life and can even lengthen it.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">How</b><span>alongside every other treatment</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Best for</b><span>everyone, from diagnosis on</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">Often missed</b><span>pain and nutrition in India</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p>Many of the biggest gains in cancer care came from controlling side effects. Modern anti-sickness drugs mean most people no longer vomit through chemotherapy. G-CSF injections push the marrow to make neutrophils faster, shortening the infection danger window.</p><p><strong>Palliative care</strong> means expert control of pain, breathlessness, nausea, low mood and other symptoms. A landmark 2010 trial in advanced lung cancer found that patients who saw a palliative care team early felt better and lived nearly three months longer.</p><p>In India, oral morphine for cancer pain is cheap and effective but often hard to obtain. Ask for a palliative care referral at any stage.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>Supportive &amp; palliative care</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 136" role="img" aria-label="Why chemotherapy causes vomiting: chemo irritates the gut, gut cells release serotonin, the vagus nerve carries the signal to the vomiting centre in the brainstem. Ondansetron blocks serotonin's receptor on the nerve; aprepitant blocks the NK1 receptor in the brain."><defs><marker id="anz" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker><marker id="bnz" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="8" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse"><path class="mk bad" d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z"/></marker></defs><rect class="box" x="10" y="30" width="160" height="56" rx="10"/><text class="t b" x="90.0" y="55.0" text-anchor="middle">Chemo</text><text   <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>G-CSF: bone ache as the marrow works overtime</li><li>Ondansetron: constipation, headache</li><li>Steroids: sleeplessness, raised sugar</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Under-used, especially outside big city hospitals</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">Used in</span><h3 class="sec">Where it fits</h3></div><div class="used"><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#oral">Mouth</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#cervical">Cervix</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#lung">Lung</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#colorectal">Colorectal</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#stomach">Stomach</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#liver">Liver</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a><a class="u2" href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#skin">Skin</a></div><p class="prose" style="font-size:14.5px;color:var(--ink-soft)">Filled: a main treatment. Outlined: used in some cases. Links open The Cancer Atlas.</p>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">The molecules</span><h3 class="sec">Key drugs in this class</h3></div><div class="mol"><article class="mc" data-cls="support" data-q="filgrastim / pegfilgrastim neupogen / neulasta g-csf (boosts neutrophils) amgen · biosimilars breast blood"><h4>Filgrastim / pegfilgrastim</h4><span class="br">Neupogen / Neulasta</span><div class="row"><span class="cl">Supportive care</span><span class="rt">Injection</span></div><p class="tg"><b>Target:</b> G-CSF (boosts neutrophils)</p><p class="nt">Makes chemo safer by shortening the infection window.</p><div class="row cx"><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a></div><p class="meta">first approved 1991 · Amgen · biosimilars</p></article><article class="mc" data-cls="support" data-q="ondansetron zofran serotonin 5-ht3 receptor novartis (gsk) · generic "><h4>Ondansetron</h4><span class="br">Zofran</span><div class="row"><span class="cl">Supportive care</span><span class="rt">Pill / IV</span></div><p class="tg"><b>Target:</b> Serotonin 5-HT3 receptor</p><p class="nt">Made chemo nausea manageable for most people.</p><p class="meta">first approved 1991 · Novartis (GSK) · generic</p></article><article class="mc" data-cls="support" data-q="aprepitant emend nk1 receptor in the brain msd · generic "><h4>Aprepitant</h4><span class="br">Emend</span><div class="row"><span class="cl">Supportive care</span><span class="rt">Pill</span></div><p class="tg"><b>Target:</b> NK1 receptor in the brain</p><p class="meta">first approved 2003 · MSD · generic</p></article><article class="mc" data-cls="support" data-q="zoledronic acid / denosumab zometa / xgeva bone-eating cells novartis / amgen breast prostate blood"><h4>Zoledronic acid / denosumab</h4><span class="br">Zometa / Xgeva</span><div class="row"><span class="cl">Supportive care</span><span class="rt">IV / injection</span></div><p class="tg"><b>Target:</b> Bone-eating cells</p><p class="nt">Protect bones from cancer spread.</p><div class="row cx"><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#breast">Breast</a><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#prostate">Prostate</a><a href="/blog/explainers/the-cancer-atlas/#blood">Blood</a></div><p class="meta">first approved 2001 · Novartis / Amgen</p></article></div>
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">Palliative care is only for the last days of life.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>It works best from diagnosis, alongside treatment aimed at cure.</p></div>
  <div class="pn"><a href="#transplant">← Stem cell transplant</a><a href="#frontier">The frontier →</a></div>
</section>
<section class="view" id="v-frontier" data-view="frontier" hidden>
  <header class="c-head">
    <p class="eyebrow">Chapter 10 of 10 · What is new in 2025 and 2026 · since now</p>
    <h2>The frontier</h2>
    <p class="thesis">Drugs that destroy proteins rather than block them, the first medicine against RAS in pancreatic cancer, vaccines written for one patient's tumor, and blood tests that find cancer DNA.</p>
  </header>
  <div class="stats"><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">2026 first</b><span>PROTAC degrader approved (vepdegestrant)</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">2026 first</b><span>RAS drug for pancreatic cancer (daraxonrasib)</span></div><div class="stat"><b style="font-size:18px">2026 first</b><span>ctDNA blood test required for a solid-tumor approval</span></div></div>
  <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">1 · How it works</span><h3 class="sec">The mechanism</h3></div>
  <div class="prose"><p><strong>Protein degraders (PROTACs).</strong> A normal inhibitor must sit on its target to block it. A PROTAC instead tethers the target to the cell's tagging machinery, so the cell shreds it, and the PROTAC moves on to the next copy. In 2026 vepdegestrant became the first approved, for oestrogen-receptor breast cancer with ESR1 mutations.</p><p><strong>Hitting RAS.</strong> RAS genes are mutated in about a quarter of all cancers and in over 9 in 10 pancreatic cancers. Daraxonrasib, approved in 2026, grabs the active form of many RAS variants; in its pancreatic trial median survival was 13.2 months versus 6.7 on chemotherapy.</p><p><strong>Personalised mRNA vaccines.</strong> The tumor's DNA is read, its unique mutated proteins are chosen by computer, and an mRNA vaccine is made for that one patient to train T cells. Trials in melanoma and other cancers are under way, combined with checkpoint inhibitors.</p><p><strong>Liquid biopsy.</strong> Tumors shed fragments of DNA into the blood (ctDNA). Tests can now detect cancer left after surgery. In 2026 atezolizumab was approved after bladder cancer surgery specifically for patients with ctDNA in their blood, a first for solid tumors.</p><p>Also new in 2026: a modified herpes virus that infects melanoma (Tudriqev), and Keytruda as a shot under the skin (Keytruda Qlex, approved late 2025) ahead of its 2028 patent expiry.</p></div>
  <figure class="plate"><div class="fig-tag"><b>Fig 1</b>The frontier</div><div class="scroll" class="minw-600"><svg viewBox="0 0 770 190" role="img" aria-label="How a PROTAC works: it bridges the   <div class="sec-row"><span class="mono-l">2 · The cost to the body</span><h3 class="sec">Side effects, and why they happen</h3></div>
  <div class="two">
    <div class="box-l"><h4>Common side effects</h4><ul><li>Too new for long-term data; watch for updates</li></ul></div>
    <div class="box-l ok" style="border-color:var(--hema)"><h4>Limits and resistance</h4><ul><li>Prices are very high at launch</li><li>Most results so far are from advanced cancer; benefits in early disease are still being tested</li></ul></div>
  </div>
  
  
  <div class="myth"><span class="k kq">Myth</span><p class="mq">A cure for cancer is one breakthrough away.</p><span class="k kf">Fact</span><p>Cancer is hundreds of diseases. Progress comes drug by drug and type by type, and it is real: death rates in many countries have fallen for three decades.</p></div>

<script>
(function(){
  const views = Array.from(document.querySelectorAll('.view'));
  const ids = views.map(v => v.dataset.view);
  const bar = document.getElementById('barin');
  function show(){
    let h = (location.hash || '').slice(1);
    if (!ids.includes(h)) h = 'arsenal';
    views.forEach(v => { v.hidden = v.dataset.view !== h; });
    bar.querySelectorAll('a').forEach(a => {
      const on = a.getAttribute('href') === '#' + h;
      if (on) { a.setAttribute('aria-current', 'page'); try { a.scrollIntoView({block:'nearest', inline:'center'}); } catch(e){} }
      else a.removeAttribute('aria-current');
    });
    const hd = document.querySelector('#v-' + h + ' h2');
    document.title = hd ? hd.textContent + ' · The Cancer Arsenal' : 'The Cancer Arsenal';
  }
  window.addEventListener('hashchange', () => { show(); window.scrollTo(0, 0); });
  show();

  // decoder
  const SFX = [["-mab", "monoclonal antibody"], ["-ximab / -zumab / -umab", "mouse-human / humanised / fully human antibody (older naming)"], ["-nib", "small-molecule kinase inhibitor (-tinib: tyrosine kinase)"], ["-ciclib", "CDK inhibitor"], ["-parib", "PARP inhibitor"], ["-rasib", "RAS inhibitor"], ["-lisib", "PI3K inhibitor"], ["-toclax", "BCL-2 inhibitor"], ["-zomib", "proteasome inhibitor"], ["-platin", "platinum chemotherapy"], ["-taxel", "taxane spindle poison"], ["-rubicin", "anthracycline"], ["-tecan", "topoisomerase I poison (also ADC payloads)"], ["-vedotin / -deruxtecan / -govitecan", "the payload of an antibody-drug conjugate"], ["-rozole", "aromatase inhibitor"], ["-lutamide", "androgen receptor blocker"], ["-relin / -relix", "GnRH agonist / antagonist"], ["-cel", "cell therapy"], ["-vec / -repvec", "virus-based gene therapy"], ["-gestrant / -estrant", "oestrogen receptor degrader"]];
  const din = document.getElementById('dec-in'), dout = document.getElementById('dec-out');
  function decode(){
    const w = din.value.trim().toLowerCase();
    if (!w) { dout.textContent = ''; return; }
    let best = null;
    if (/mab$/.test(w)) best = ['mab', 'monoclonal antibody'];
    else SFX.forEach(([k, d]) => k.split('/').map(s => s.trim().replace(/^-/, '')).forEach(s => {
      if (s && w.endsWith(s) && (!best || s.length > best[0].length)) best = [s, d];
    }));
    dout.innerHTML = '';
    if (best) {
      const b = document.createElement('b'); b.textContent = '-' + best[0];
      dout.append(b, document.createTextNode(' → ' + best[1]));
      if (/zumab$/.test(w)) dout.append(document.createTextNode(' (humanised)'));
      else if (/ximab$/.test(w)) dout.append(document.createTextNode(' (part mouse, part human)'));
      else if (/umab$/.test(w)) dout.append(document.createTextNode(' (fully human)'));
    } else dout.textContent = 'No standard ending found. Older drugs (like tamoxifen or cisplatin) often predate the naming system.';
  }
  din.addEventListener('input', decode); decode();

  // library filter
  const q = document.getElementById('lib-q'), cs = document.getElementById('lib-c'), n = document.getElementById('lib-n');
  const cards = Array.from(document.querySelectorAll('#lib-grid .mc'));
  let cls = 'all';
  function filt(){
    const t = q.value.trim().toLowerCase(); let c = 0;
    cards.forEach(el => { const ok = (cls === 'all' || el.dataset.cls === cls) && (!t || el.dataset.q.includes(t) || el.querySelector('h4').textContent.toLowerCase().includes(t)); el.hidden = !ok; if (ok) c++; });
    n.textContent = c + ' of ' + cards.length + ' shown';
  }
  q.addEventListener('input', filt);
  cs.addEventListener('click', e => { const b = e.target.closest('button'); if (!b) return; cls = b.dataset.c; cs.querySelectorAll('button').forEach(x => x.setAttribute('aria-pressed', String(x === b))); filt(); });
  filt();
})();
</script>
